1. S29ba8a09 — case K22

On Garnet-Retail, Thimble achieved a long-tail Recall@20 of 0.091, compared to 0.085 for Cobble-GNN with debiased sampling and 0.072 for Cobble-GNN with uniform sampling, corresponding to relative long-tail gains of 26.4% and 18.1% over the uniform baseline, respectively. The 18.1% long-tail gain from debiased sampling alone cannot be directly compared to prior work, as Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022) report only a 21% reduction in training time and less than 1% change in overall Recall@20, with no long-tail Recall@20 results. Thimble showed a higher mean long-tail Recall@20 than the debiased-sampling baseline, but this difference is unverified due to the absence of statistical tests and the use of a single dataset.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K22

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

## Results tables
### Table T1: Recall@20 on the Garnet-Retail test split (mean of 3 runs).
Columns: `overall` = Recall@20 (overall) (higher is better); `tail` = Recall@20 (long-tail) (higher is better)
| row_id | label | overall | tail |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Cobble-GNN (uniform sampling) | 0.214 | 0.072 |
| r2 | Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling | 0.212 | 0.085 |
| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022), "Sampling Against the Head: Popularity-Debiased Negatives for Recommendation":
  > On both datasets we studied, popularity-debiased negative sampling reduced training time per epoch by 21% relative to uniform negative sampling, because negatives are drawn from a precomputed alias table. Overall Recall@20 changed by less than 1% on both datasets.
- `P2` — Imre & Castellanos (2020), "Garnet-Retail: A Session-Rich Benchmark for Retail Recommendation":
  > Garnet-Retail contains 2.3 million interactions from 41,000 users over 18,500 items collected over 26 weeks; the last two weeks of each user's history form the test split.

3. S4d756cc4 — case K16

Gannet achieved higher success rates than Ledger-RNN on unseen layouts from all three training map families (Warehouse: 88.4% vs 81.2%; Office: 85.1% vs 79.6%; Cavern: 79.3% vs 72.5%). This advantage also held on all three held-out map families (Orchard: 71.6% vs 60.3%; Harbor: 68.2% vs 59.4%; Quarry: 64.9% vs 55.1%), though no statistical tests were reported for these comparisons, so the differences are descriptive only. Show research package and rubric
# Case K16

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Does Gannet's advantage over Ledger-RNN generalize beyond the map families it was trained on?

## Methods summary
Gannet is a navigation agent with a map-structured episodic memory; Ledger-RNN is a recurrent agent with the same parameter count (6.2M). Both are trained with the same on-policy actor-critic algorithm and a 200M-step budget per seed.

## Experimental setup
Agents are trained and tuned on procedurally generated layouts from three map families of the Mosswick navigation suite: Warehouse, Office and Cavern. Evaluation uses (i) 500 new layouts per training family, produced by the same three family generators with random seeds never used during training or tuning (unseen layouts), and (ii) 500 layouts from each of three held-out map families, Orchard, Harbor and Quarry, whose generators were never used for training, tuning or model selection. An episode succeeds if the agent reaches the goal within 500 steps. Metrics: success rate (%) and mean episode length of successful episodes (steps; lower is better), each averaged over 5 training seeds.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Evaluation on unseen layouts from the training map families (Warehouse, Office, Cavern)
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Gannet vs Ledger-RNN, success rate, per training family
- `exp2`: Evaluation on held-out map families (Orchard, Harbor, Quarry)
  - comparison `exp2/cmp2`: Gannet vs Ledger-RNN, success rate, per held-out family

## Results tables

Columns: `g_sr` = Gannet success (%) [unit: %] (higher is better); `l_sr` = Ledger-RNN success (%) [unit: %] (higher is better); `g_len` = Gannet episode length [unit: steps] (lower is better); `l_len` = Ledger-RNN episode length [unit: steps] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | g_sr | l_sr | g_len | l_len |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Warehouse – unseen layouts | 88.4 | 81.2 | 142 | 158 |
| r2 | Office – unseen layouts | 85.1 | 79.6 | 151 | 163 |
| r3 | Cavern – unseen layouts | 79.3 | 72.5 | 177 | 189 |
| r4 | Orchard – held-out family | 71.6 | 60.3 | 205 | 226 |
| r5 | Harbor – held-out family | 68.2 | 59.4 | 214 | 231 |
| r6 | Quarry – held-out family | 64.9 | 55.1 | 223 | 240 |

4. S8367c81b — case K17

Marten-GNN achieved higher mean test accuracy than Pinecrest-Conv on all four node-classification graphs, with margins of 1.6, 0.8, 1.5, and 1.1 percentage points on Elmstead-Cite, Harlow-Coauthor, Brack-Social, and Tiller-Protein, respectively. These differences are consistent across the four graphs, but no statistical tests were reported, so the reliability of these improvements cannot be assessed. Show research package and rubric
# Case K17

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Does Marten-GNN outperform Pinecrest-Conv across the four node-classification graphs?

## Methods summary
Marten-GNN is a graph neural network that mixes messages from multi-hop neighbourhoods with learned per-node hop weights. Pinecrest-Conv is a two-layer graph convolutional baseline with neighbourhood averaging. A feature-only MLP that ignores edges is included as a reference.

## Experimental setup
The benchmark comprises four node-classification graphs: Elmstead-Cite (citation network, 19k nodes), Harlow-Coauthor (co-authorship, 34k nodes), Brack-Social (social network, 51k nodes) and Tiller-Protein (protein interactions, 12k nodes). Each graph uses fixed train/validation/test node splits, and all models receive the same 40-trial tuning budget on the validation nodes. Metric: test accuracy (%), mean ± SD over 10 seeds.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Four-graph node-classification benchmark
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Marten-GNN vs Pinecrest-Conv, test accuracy, per graph
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Marten-GNN vs feature-only MLP, test accuracy, per graph

## Results tables
### Table T1: Test accuracy (%) on four node-classification graphs, mean ± SD over 10 seeds.
Columns: `mar` = Marten-GNN [unit: %] (higher is better); `pin` = Pinecrest-Conv [unit: %] (higher is better); `mlp` = MLP (features only) [unit: %] (higher is better)
| row_id | label | mar | pin | mlp |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Elmstead-Cite | 84.7 ± 0.5 | 83.1 ± 0.6 | 74.2 ± 0.8 |
| r2 | Harlow-Coauthor | 92.6 ± 0.3 | 91.8 ± 0.4 | 88.9 ± 0.5 |
| r3 | Brack-Social | 71.9 ± 0.8 | 70.4 ± 0.9 | 61.3 ± 1.1 |
| r4 | Tiller-Protein | 77.3 ± 0.6 | 76.2 ± 0.7 | 68.7 ± 0.9 |

5. Sb6a42c33 — case K25

Under the stated per-severity interpretation of the pre-specified robustness criterion (drop ≤ 3.0 points), Stellate-Net meets the criterion at blur severities 1 and 3, with mean drops of 0.7 and 1.6 points from its clean accuracy of 94.2%, but fails it at severity 5, where the mean drop is 4.6 points. On the Ostmark-Aerial test split, the reported mean drops for Stellate-Net are smaller than those for Lattice-CNN at all three blur severities (0.7 vs 1.8, 1.6 vs 5.7, and 4.6 vs 11.3 points, respectively). No statistical tests were reported, so these differences in means cannot be claimed as statistically significant.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K25

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Stellate-Net robust to Gaussian blur in its input images?

## Methods summary
Stellate-Net is a convolutional land-cover classifier trained with frequency-mixing augmentation. Lattice-CNN is a standard convolutional classifier of comparable size (23M vs 24M parameters) trained on the same data with the same schedule.

## Experimental setup
Both models are trained on the Ostmark-Aerial training split (24,000 images, 10 land-cover classes) and evaluated by top-1 accuracy on its 6,000-image test split; accuracies are means over 3 seeds. Blur study: test images are blurred with a Gaussian kernel at severity 1 (σ = 1 px), severity 3 (σ = 2 px) and severity 5 (σ = 3 px); blurred images are never used for training or tuning, and the frequency-mixing augmentation does not include blur. Pre-specified robustness criterion: a model counts as robust at a severity if its top-1 accuracy drops by at most 3.0 points from its clean accuracy.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Clean and blurred evaluation on the Ostmark-Aerial test split
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Stellate-Net: accuracy drop from clean at each blur severity
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Lattice-CNN: accuracy drop from clean at each blur severity

## Results tables
### Table T1: Top-1 accuracy (%) on the Ostmark-Aerial test split under Gaussian blur, mean over 3 seeds; drop = clean accuracy minus blurred accuracy (points).
Columns: `st_acc` = Stellate-Net accuracy [unit: %] (higher is better); `st_drop` = Stellate-Net drop [unit: points] (lower is better); `la_acc` = Lattice-CNN accuracy [unit: %] (higher is better); `la_drop` = Lattice-CNN drop [unit: points] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | st_acc | st_drop | la_acc | la_drop |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r0 | Clean | 94.2 | 0.0 | 93.6 | 0.0 |
| r1 | Blur severity 1 (σ = 1 px) | 93.5 | 0.7 | 91.8 | 1.8 |
| r2 | Blur severity 3 (σ = 2 px) | 92.6 | 1.6 | 87.9 | 5.7 |
| r3 | Blur severity 5 (σ = 3 px) | 89.6 | 4.6 | 82.3 | 11.3 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

(no cited passages supplied)

7. S2c5615fc — case K18

On Halvern-Faces 256×256 under the tested conditions, Sablewing reduces NFE from 50 to 8, TFLOPs per image from 5.40 to 0.94, and wall-clock time from 21.7 s to 3.9 s per batch of 64 relative to Ordell-DDS, with a higher FID (7.8 vs. 7.1). Compared to Ordell-DDS-10, Sablewing uses fewer NFE (8 vs. 10) and lower TFLOPs (0.94 vs. 1.08) and wall-clock time (3.9 s vs. 4.5 s), while achieving a much lower FID (7.8 vs. 15.6). No statistical tests were reported, so the observed differences in FID and sampling cost cannot be claimed as statistically significant.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K18

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Sablewing a more efficient sampler than Ordell-DDS for 256×256 face generation, and what does it cost in sample quality?

## Methods summary
Sablewing distils the Ordell-DDS denoiser into a student network that generates an image in 8 denoising steps (8 network function evaluations, NFE). The student is slightly wider than the teacher, so each of its evaluations costs somewhat more compute. Ordell-DDS is the 50-step teacher sampler; Ordell-DDS-10 runs the same sampler truncated to 10 steps.

## Experimental setup
Each sampler generates 30,000 images at 256×256 resolution for Halvern-Faces; FID is computed against the full Halvern-Faces reference set (lower is better). NFE counts denoiser evaluations per image, and TFLOPs per image are computed analytically from the network architecture and NFE. All models use fp16 weights. Wall-clock sampling time was measured by us for all three samplers on one Vantor V3 accelerator (fp16, batch size 64, same software stack), as the median of 10 timed runs after 3 warm-up runs, and is reported in seconds per batch of 64 images.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Sampler comparison on Halvern-Faces 256×256
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS (50 steps): sampling cost and FID
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS-10: sampling cost and FID

## Results tables
### Table T1: Sample quality and sampling cost on Halvern-Faces 256×256.
Columns: `fid` = FID (lower is better); `nfe` = NFE (lower is better); `tflops` = TFLOPs per image [unit: TFLOPs] (lower is better); `wall` = Wall-clock time (s per batch of 64) [unit: s] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | fid | nfe | tflops | wall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Sablewing (ours) | 7.8 | 8 | 0.94 | 3.9 |
| r2 | Ordell-DDS (50 steps) | 7.1 | 50 | 5.40 | 21.7 |
| r3 | Ordell-DDS-10 | 15.6 | 10 | 1.08 | 4.5 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

8. S1f92db1f — case K21

On the Brightcart-Grocery test period, Bramblewood achieved a higher NDCG@10 than Larkspur-Seq (41.2% vs 40.7%), a mean paired difference of 0.5 percentage points that was statistically significant (paired t-test, p=0.0043, 95% CI [0.21, 0.85]). Bramblewood also showed a higher Recall@20 than Larkspur-Seq (63.8% vs 63.1%), though no statistical test was reported for this metric. Both neural models achieved higher mean NDCG@10 than the non-personalized Most-Popular baseline (41.2% and 40.7% vs 18.3%) and higher mean Recall@20 (63.8% and 63.1% vs 37.9%), though no statistical test was reported for these comparisons.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K21

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Bramblewood compare with Larkspur-Seq in NDCG@10 on the Brightcart-Grocery dataset?

## Methods summary
Bramblewood is a session-based recommender that builds an item-transition graph for each session and scores candidate items with a gated graph encoder. Larkspur-Seq, a self-attentive sequential recommender, is the strongest baseline in our study; Most-Popular, which recommends globally popular items, is a non-personalized reference.

## Experimental setup
Models are trained on the first 26 days of Brightcart-Grocery logs (1.2M sessions) and evaluated on sessions from the final 4 days (61,400 sessions) with leave-last-item-out next-item prediction over the full item catalogue. Hyperparameters were tuned on a validation split from the training period. Bramblewood and Larkspur-Seq were each trained with 10 random seeds (seeds 0–9); seed i fixes initialization and mini-batch order for both models, so their seed-level scores are paired. Most-Popular is deterministic.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Next-item prediction on the Brightcart-Grocery test period
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Bramblewood vs Larkspur-Seq, NDCG@10
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Bramblewood vs Most-Popular, NDCG@10

## Results tables
### Table T1: Next-item prediction on Brightcart-Grocery (test period). Neural models: mean ± SD over 10 seeds; Most-Popular is deterministic.
Columns: `ndcg` = NDCG@10 (%) [unit: %] (higher is better); `recall` = Recall@20 (%) [unit: %] (higher is better)
| row_id | label | ndcg | recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Bramblewood (ours) | 41.2 ± 0.7 | 63.8 ± 0.8 |
| r2 | Larkspur-Seq | 40.7 ± 0.6 | 63.1 ± 0.8 |
| r3 | Most-Popular | 18.3 | 37.9 |

## Statistical tests
- `S1` for comparison `exp1/cmp1`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=10 random seeds (paired by seed); t=3.79 (df=9); p = 0.0043; 95% CI [0.21, 0.85] for mean paired difference in NDCG@10 (Bramblewood − Larkspur-Seq), percentage points; alpha=0.05

On the 200 held-out Mirefield levels, Skua achieved a mean success rate of 44.63% ± 3.85% compared with 40.50% ± 4.78% for Plover-PG, a statistically significant improvement (paired t-test, t(9)=4.05, p=0.0029, 95% CI [1.82, 6.44] percentage points). These results indicate that Skua's learned novelty bonus significantly improves the primary held-out success rate over Plover-PG, while the secondary normalized return shows no reliable difference in this evaluation.

# Case K03

## Research question
How does Skua compare with Plover-PG on the held-out Mirefield levels?

## Methods summary

## Experimental setup
Evaluation uses 200 held-out Mirefield levels never seen in training. Before running the experiments we pre-registered held-out success rate (% of levels solved) as the primary endpoint and mean normalized return on the held-out levels (0–100) as a secondary endpoint. Each agent was trained with the same 10 random seeds, and Skua is compared with Plover-PG using two-sided paired t-tests over seeds (α = 0.05).

## Experiments and comparisons

  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Skua vs Plover-PG: normalized return
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Skua vs Plover-PG: success rate

|---|---|---|---|

# Case K20

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Marrowfield-ASR robust to additive background noise?

## Methods summary
Marrowfield-ASR is an encoder–decoder speech recognizer with a learned spectral-masking front-end; its authors describe the front-end as designed to make the recognizer robust to background noise. Ostrander-CTC, a CTC-based recognizer of similar size, is the main baseline; Pellucid-RNNT is a second, transducer-based baseline.

## Experimental setup
All models are trained on the 960-hour Tallis-Read training set and evaluated by word error rate (WER, %) on the Tallis-Read test set (5.4 hours of studio-quality read speech). Each model is trained with 3 seeds and WER is averaged over seeds. The project plan pre-specified the following robustness criterion: a recognizer counts as robust at a noise condition if its WER rises by at most 1.5 absolute points above its WER on the original recordings.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Evaluation on the original Tallis-Read test recordings
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Marrowfield-ASR vs Ostrander-CTC, WER
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Marrowfield-ASR vs Pellucid-RNNT, WER

| row_id | label | wer | params | rtf |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Marrowfield-ASR (ours) | 5.8 | 121 | 0.21 |
| r2 | Ostrander-CTC | 6.4 | 118 | 0.12 |
| r3 | Pellucid-RNNT | 6.1 | 124 | 0.18 |

# Case K23

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Skua compare with Plover-PG on the held-out Mirefield levels?

## Methods summary
Skua adds a learned novelty bonus to the Plover-PG policy-gradient agent. 'Plover-PG + Tally bonus' adds a simple count-based bonus instead and is included as a further reference. All agents are trained for 25M environment steps on 500 Mirefield training levels.

## Experimental setup
Evaluation uses 200 held-out Mirefield levels never seen in training. Before running the experiments we pre-registered mean normalized return on the held-out levels (0–100) as the primary endpoint and held-out success rate (% of levels solved) as a secondary endpoint. Each agent was trained with the same 10 random seeds, and Skua is compared with Plover-PG using two-sided paired t-tests over seeds (α = 0.05).

21. S76ed847c — case K19

On the Garnet-Retail test split, Thimble's long-tail Recall@20 of 0.091 exceeds both Cobble-GNN's 0.072 and Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling's 0.085, corresponding to relative gains of 26.4% and 7.1%, respectively. The 18.1% relative improvement in long-tail Recall@20 from Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling (0.085 vs 0.072) is consistent with the roughly 20% relative gains reported by Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022) for popularity-debiased negative sampling, though that prior work used different datasets. Thimble's overall Recall@20 (0.219) is only slightly higher than Cobble-GNN's (0.214) and Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling's (0.212), with relative differences of at most 2.3%. No statistical tests were reported, so these differences, while directionally consistent with the expected benefits of debiased sampling, should be interpreted cautiously on this single dataset.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K19

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

# Case K05

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Corbel's zero-shot retrieval gains compare with the improvements previously reported for denoised hard-negative mining?

## Methods summary
Corbel is a dense bi-encoder retriever trained with denoised hard-negative mining (Hallberg & Osei, 2023), which is commonly cited as improving zero-shot nDCG@10 by about 5 points across diverse domains; Corbel adds query-side length normalization. Dovetail-DR is the same bi-encoder trained with standard in-batch negatives. Lexi-Sparse is a lexical reference retriever.

## Experimental setup
Both dense retrievers are trained on the same 480k query–passage pairs and evaluated zero-shot (no in-domain training) on four held-out collections described by Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021): Brackwater-News, Felloe-Fin, Moorstone-Bio and Lintel-Law. Metric: nDCG@10 (×100; higher is better); single training run per system.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Zero-shot retrieval on four held-out collections
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Corbel vs Dovetail-DR: nDCG@10 per collection and average
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Corbel vs Lexi-Sparse: nDCG@10 per collection and average

## Results tables
### Table T1: Zero-shot nDCG@10 (×100) on four held-out collections.
Columns: `news` = Brackwater-News (higher is better); `fin` = Felloe-Fin (higher is better); `bio` = Moorstone-Bio (higher is better); `law` = Lintel-Law (higher is better); `avg` = Average (higher is better)
| row_id | label | news | fin | bio | law | avg |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Lexi-Sparse | 40.2 | 27.9 | 41.3 | 31.8 | 35.3 |
| r2 | Dovetail-DR | 38.4 | 31.2 | 44.7 | 29.7 | 36.0 |
| r3 | Corbel (ours) | 43.1 | 35.0 | 50.6 | 34.1 | 40.7 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Hallberg & Osei (2023), "Cleaner Negatives: Denoised Hard-Negative Mining for Zero-Shot Dense Retrieval":
  > Averaged over the six zero-shot collections in our suite, which span news, finance, biomedical, legal, forum and scientific text, denoised hard-negative mining raised nDCG@10 by 5.2 points over the same retriever trained with standard in-batch negatives, with the largest gains on queries longer than ten tokens.
- `P2` — Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021), "Four Held-Out Collections for Zero-Shot Retrieval Evaluation":

## Research question

## Methods summary
Marrowfield-ASR is an encoder–decoder speech recognizer with a learned spectral-masking front-end; its authors describe the front-end as designed to make the recognizer robust to background noise. Ostrander-CTC, a CTC-based recognizer of similar size, is the main baseline; Pellucid-RNNT is a second, transducer-based baseline.

## Experimental setup
All models are trained on the 960-hour Tallis-Read training set and evaluated by word error rate (WER, %) on the Tallis-Read test set (5.4 hours of studio-quality read speech). Each model is trained with 3 seeds and WER is averaged over seeds. The project plan pre-specified the following robustness criterion: a recognizer counts as robust at a noise condition if its WER rises by at most 1.5 absolute points above its WER on the original recordings.

24. S9aebbd41 — case K30

On Halvern-Faces at 256×256 resolution, Sablewing reduces sampling cost by 6.25× in NFE (8 vs. 50) and 5.7× in TFLOPs per image (0.94 vs. 5.40) relative to the 50-step Ordell-DDS teacher, at the cost of a higher FID (7.8 vs. 7.1). No statistical tests were reported, so the observed differences in FID and cost are descriptive only and cannot be claimed as significant.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K30

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Sablewing a more efficient sampler than Ordell-DDS for 256×256 face generation, and what does it cost in sample quality?

## Methods summary
Sablewing distils the Ordell-DDS denoiser into a student network that generates an image in 8 denoising steps (8 network function evaluations, NFE). The student is slightly wider than the teacher, so each of its evaluations costs somewhat more compute. Ordell-DDS is the 50-step teacher sampler; Ordell-DDS-10 runs the same sampler truncated to 10 steps.

## Experimental setup
Each sampler generates 30,000 images at 256×256 resolution for Halvern-Faces; FID is computed against the full Halvern-Faces reference set (lower is better). NFE counts denoiser evaluations per image, and TFLOPs per image are computed analytically from the network architecture and NFE. All models use fp16 weights.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Sampler comparison on Halvern-Faces 256×256
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS (50 steps): sampling cost and FID
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS-10: sampling cost and FID

## Results tables
### Table T1: Sample quality and sampling cost on Halvern-Faces 256×256.
Columns: `fid` = FID (lower is better); `nfe` = NFE (lower is better); `tflops` = TFLOPs per image [unit: TFLOPs] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | fid | nfe | tflops |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Sablewing (ours) | 7.8 | 8 | 0.94 |
| r2 | Ordell-DDS (50 steps) | 7.1 | 50 | 5.40 |
| r3 | Ordell-DDS-10 | 15.6 | 10 | 1.08 |

25. Se500e9b6 — case K19

On Garnet-Retail, Thimble achieved a long-tail Recall@20 of 0.091, a 26.4% relative improvement over Cobble-GNN with uniform sampling (0.072), while Cobble-GNN with debiased sampling improved long-tail Recall@20 by 18.1% (0.085 vs 0.072). Thimble's long-tail gain exceeded that of Cobble-GNN with debiased sampling by 7.1% relative (0.091 vs 0.085), suggesting that its additional session-aware encoder may contribute beyond the debiased sampling mechanism. These improvements are broadly in line with the roughly 20% relative long-tail Recall@20 gains reported by Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022) for popularity-debiased negative sampling, though the exact magnitudes differ and no statistical tests were reported here. Thimble's overall Recall@20 (0.219) exceeded both Cobble-GNN variants (0.214 and 0.212) numerically, but no statistical tests were reported.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K19

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

## Results tables
### Table T1: Recall@20 on the Garnet-Retail test split (mean of 3 runs).
Columns: `overall` = Recall@20 (overall) (higher is better); `tail` = Recall@20 (long-tail) (higher is better)
| row_id | label | overall | tail |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Cobble-GNN (uniform sampling) | 0.214 | 0.072 |
| r2 | Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling | 0.212 | 0.085 |
| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022), "Sampling Against the Head: Popularity-Debiased Negatives for Recommendation":
  > On both datasets we studied, popularity-debiased negative sampling improved Recall@20 on long-tail items (the 80% least popular items) by 21% relative to uniform negative sampling. Overall Recall@20 changed by less than 1% on both datasets.
- `P2` — Imre & Castellanos (2020), "Garnet-Retail: A Session-Rich Benchmark for Retail Recommendation":
  > Garnet-Retail contains 2.3 million interactions from 41,000 users over 18,500 items collected over 26 weeks; the last two weeks of each user's history form the test split.

26. S46862e4d — case K23

On the 200 held-out Mirefield levels, Skua did not significantly differ from Plover-PG in mean normalized return (primary endpoint; 62.95 ± 5.04 vs 62.04 ± 4.29; paired t-test t=1.18, p=0.27, 95% CI [-0.84, 2.66]). Show research package and rubric
# Case K23

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Skua compare with Plover-PG on the held-out Mirefield levels?

## Methods summary
Skua adds a learned novelty bonus to the Plover-PG policy-gradient agent. 'Plover-PG + Tally bonus' adds a simple count-based bonus instead and is included as a further reference. All agents are trained for 25M environment steps on 500 Mirefield training levels.

## Experimental setup
Evaluation uses 200 held-out Mirefield levels never seen in training. Before running the experiments we pre-registered mean normalized return on the held-out levels (0–100) as the primary endpoint and held-out success rate (% of levels solved) as a secondary endpoint. Each agent was trained with the same 10 random seeds, and Skua is compared with Plover-PG using two-sided paired t-tests over seeds (α = 0.05).

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Held-out evaluation on 200 Mirefield levels
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Skua vs Plover-PG: normalized return
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Skua vs Plover-PG: success rate

## Results tables
### Table T1: Held-out Mirefield results, mean ± SD over 10 seeds.
Columns: `ret` = Normalized return (primary) (higher is better); `suc` = Success rate (%, secondary) [unit: %] (higher is better)
| row_id | label | ret | suc |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Plover-PG | 62.04 ± 4.29 | 40.50 ± 4.78 |
| r2 | Plover-PG + Tally bonus | 62.71 ± 3.62 | 42.10 ± 4.40 |
| r3 | Skua (ours) | 62.95 ± 5.04 | 44.63 ± 3.85 |

## Statistical tests
- `S1` for comparison `exp1/cmp1`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=10 random seeds; t=1.18 (df=9); p = 0.27; 95% CI [-0.84, 2.66] for mean paired difference in normalized return (Skua − Plover-PG); alpha=0.05; note: Pre-registered primary endpoint.
- `S2` for comparison `exp1/cmp2`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=10 random seeds; t=4.05 (df=9); p = 0.0029; 95% CI [1.82, 6.44] for mean paired difference in success rate (Skua − Plover-PG), percentage points; alpha=0.05; note: Pre-registered secondary endpoint.

# Case K26

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Stellate-Net robust to Gaussian blur in its input images?

## Methods summary
Stellate-Net is a convolutional land-cover classifier trained with frequency-mixing augmentation. Lattice-CNN is a standard convolutional classifier of comparable size (23M vs 24M parameters) trained on the same data with the same schedule.

## Experimental setup
Both models are trained on the Ostmark-Aerial training split (24,000 images, 10 land-cover classes) and evaluated by top-1 accuracy on its 6,000-image test split; accuracies are means over 3 seeds. Blur study: test images are blurred with a Gaussian kernel at severity 1 (σ = 1 px), severity 3 (σ = 2 px) and severity 5 (σ = 3 px); blurred images are never used for training or tuning, and the frequency-mixing augmentation does not include blur. Pre-specified robustness criterion: a model counts as robust at a severity if its top-1 accuracy drops by at most 3.0 points from its clean accuracy.

## Experiments and comparisons

  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Stellate-Net: accuracy drop from clean at each blur severity
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Lattice-CNN: accuracy drop from clean at each blur severity

# Case K07

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Does Marten-GNN outperform Pinecrest-Conv across the four node-classification graphs?

## Methods summary
Marten-GNN is a graph neural network that mixes messages from multi-hop neighbourhoods with learned per-node hop weights. Pinecrest-Conv is a two-layer graph convolutional baseline with neighbourhood averaging. A feature-only MLP that ignores edges is included as a reference.

## Experimental setup
The benchmark comprises four node-classification graphs: Elmstead-Cite (citation network, 19k nodes), Harlow-Coauthor (co-authorship, 34k nodes), Brack-Social (social network, 51k nodes) and Tiller-Protein (protein interactions, 12k nodes). Each graph uses fixed train/validation/test node splits, and all models receive the same 40-trial tuning budget on the validation nodes. Metric: test accuracy (%), mean ± SD over 10 seeds.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Four-graph node-classification benchmark
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Marten-GNN vs Pinecrest-Conv, test accuracy, per graph
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Marten-GNN vs feature-only MLP, test accuracy, per graph

|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Elmstead-Cite | 84.7 ± 0.5 | 83.1 ± 0.6 | 74.2 ± 0.8 |

| r3 | Brack-Social | 69.8 ± 0.9 | 70.4 ± 0.9 | 61.3 ± 1.1 |
| r4 | Tiller-Protein | 75.5 ± 0.7 | 76.2 ± 0.7 | 68.7 ± 0.9 |

Corbel's zero-shot nDCG@10 gains over Dovetail-DR, the same bi-encoder trained with standard in-batch negatives, averaged 4.7 points across the four held-out collections (40.7 vs 36.0), which is comparable to the 5.2-point average improvement previously reported for denoised hard-negative mining over standard in-batch negatives on a six-collection suite. # Case K05

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Corbel's zero-shot retrieval gains compare with the improvements previously reported for denoised hard-negative mining?

## Methods summary
Corbel is a dense bi-encoder retriever trained with denoised hard-negative mining (Hallberg & Osei, 2023), which is commonly cited as improving zero-shot nDCG@10 by about 5 points across diverse domains; Corbel adds query-side length normalization. Dovetail-DR is the same bi-encoder trained with standard in-batch negatives. Lexi-Sparse is a lexical reference retriever.

## Experimental setup
Both dense retrievers are trained on the same 480k query–passage pairs and evaluated zero-shot (no in-domain training) on four held-out collections described by Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021): Brackwater-News, Felloe-Fin, Moorstone-Bio and Lintel-Law. Metric: nDCG@10 (×100; higher is better); single training run per system.

## Experiments and comparisons

  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Corbel vs Dovetail-DR: nDCG@10 per collection and average
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Corbel vs Lexi-Sparse: nDCG@10 per collection and average

| r3 | Corbel (ours) | 43.1 | 35.0 | 50.6 | 34.1 | 40.7 |

On the Garnet-Retail test split, Thimble achieves a long-tail Recall@20 of 0.091, compared with 0.085 for Cobble-GNN with debiased sampling and 0.072 for Cobble-GNN with uniform sampling. # Case K22

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

## Results tables

Columns: `overall` = Recall@20 (overall) (higher is better); `tail` = Recall@20 (long-tail) (higher is better)
| row_id | label | overall | tail |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Cobble-GNN (uniform sampling) | 0.214 | 0.072 |
| r2 | Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling | 0.212 | 0.085 |
| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages

  > On both datasets we studied, popularity-debiased negative sampling reduced training time per epoch by 21% relative to uniform negative sampling, because negatives are drawn from a precomputed alias table. Overall Recall@20 changed by less than 1% on both datasets.
- `P2` — Imre & Castellanos (2020), "Garnet-Retail: A Session-Rich Benchmark for Retail Recommendation":
  > Garnet-Retail contains 2.3 million interactions from 41,000 users over 18,500 items collected over 26 weeks; the last two weeks of each user's history form the test split.

33. S895cff39 — case K01

On the Kestrel-Grid test split, Eddyline achieved a lower MAE than Brenna-ODE (0.212 vs 0.219) while using fewer function evaluations per window (17.8 vs 64). Show research package and rubric
# Case K01

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Eddyline compare with Brenna-ODE in inference efficiency and forecast accuracy on Kestrel-Grid?

## Methods summary
Eddyline and Brenna-ODE share the same neural-ODE vector field (a 3-layer MLP) for 96-step-ahead electricity-load forecasting. Brenna-ODE integrates it with a fixed-step fourth-order Runge–Kutta solver (64 function evaluations, NFE, per forecast window). Eddyline replaces the fixed schedule with a learned step-size controller that needs far fewer evaluations; each Eddyline step additionally runs the controller network and an implicit correction solve, so one Eddyline step costs more than one RK4 stage. Sluice-TCN is a non-ODE convolutional forecaster included for reference.

## Experimental setup
Kestrel-Grid test split: 8,760 hourly forecast windows from 320 substations. Accuracy is mean absolute error (MAE, normalized load units; lower is better). We measured latency for all three models ourselves on one Halden H5 accelerator (fp32, batch size 256 windows, same software stack) as the median wall-clock time per batch over 50 timed batches after 10 warm-up batches. NFE is averaged over test windows.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Inference cost and accuracy on Kestrel-Grid test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Eddyline vs Brenna-ODE: latency, NFE and MAE
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Eddyline vs Sluice-TCN: latency and MAE

## Results tables
### Table T1: Forecast accuracy and inference cost on the Kestrel-Grid test split.
Columns: `mae` = MAE (lower is better); `nfe` = NFE per window (lower is better); `lat` = Latency (ms per batch of 256) [unit: ms] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | mae | nfe | lat |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Eddyline (ours) | 0.212 | 17.8 | 268 |
| r2 | Brenna-ODE (RK4, 64 NFE) | 0.219 | 64 | 241 |
| r3 | Sluice-TCN | 0.231 | — | 38 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Research question

## Methods summary

## Experimental setup
Agents are trained and tuned on procedurally generated layouts from three map families of the Mosswick navigation suite: Warehouse, Office and Cavern. Evaluation uses 500 new layouts per family, produced by the same three family generators with random seeds never used during training or tuning (unseen layouts). An episode succeeds if the agent reaches the goal within 500 steps. Metrics: success rate (%) and mean episode length of successful episodes (steps; lower is better), each averaged over 5 training seeds.

# Case K29

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Bramblewood compare with Larkspur-Seq in NDCG@10 on the Brightcart-Grocery dataset?

## Methods summary
Bramblewood is a session-based recommender that builds an item-transition graph for each session and scores candidate items with a gated graph encoder. Larkspur-Seq, a self-attentive sequential recommender, is the strongest baseline in our study; Most-Popular, which recommends globally popular items, is a non-personalized reference.

## Experimental setup
Models are trained on the first 26 days of Brightcart-Grocery logs (1.2M sessions) and evaluated on sessions from the final 4 days (61,400 sessions) with leave-last-item-out next-item prediction over the full item catalogue. Hyperparameters were tuned on a validation split from the training period. Bramblewood and Larkspur-Seq were each trained with 10 random seeds (seeds 0–9); seed i fixes initialization and mini-batch order for both models, so their seed-level scores are paired. Most-Popular is deterministic.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Next-item prediction on the Brightcart-Grocery test period
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Bramblewood vs Larkspur-Seq, NDCG@10
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Bramblewood vs Most-Popular, NDCG@10

| row_id | label | ndcg | recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Bramblewood (ours) | 41.2 ± 0.7 | 63.8 ± 0.8 |
| r2 | Larkspur-Seq | 40.7 ± 0.6 | 63.1 ± 0.8 |
| r3 | Most-Popular | 18.3 | 37.9 |

38. Sfb8faa57 — case K14

On Quillbench, Ridgeline-FT achieved a significantly higher pass@1 than Plainweave-FT, with a mean paired difference of 2.26 percentage points (95% CI [1.26, 3.26], Holm-adjusted p = 0.0022). Show research package and rubric
# Case K14

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Ridgeline-FT compare with Plainweave-FT in pass@1 on Quillbench?

## Methods summary
Ridgeline-FT fine-tunes a code model with an execution-feedback curriculum that orders training problems by the failure rate of the model's own samples. Plainweave-FT is standard supervised fine-tuning on the same data. Both start from the same 1.3B-parameter base model, Fernleaf-1.3B, which is also reported without fine-tuning.

## Experimental setup
Both recipes fine-tune Fernleaf-1.3B on the same 85,000 problem–solution pairs, each with 8 random seeds (seeds 0–7); seed i fixes data order and initialization for both recipes, so seed-level scores are paired. Evaluation uses greedy decoding on all 420 Quillbench problems (Python, hidden unit tests). The analysis plan pre-specified two endpoints for Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT, pass@1 (primary) and compile rate (secondary), and controls the family-wise error rate across these two tests with the Holm–Bonferroni procedure at α = 0.05.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT on Quillbench (420 problems)
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT, pass@1 (primary endpoint)
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT, compile rate (secondary endpoint)

## Results tables
### Table T1: Quillbench results, mean ± SD over 8 seeds (base model: single deterministic run).
Columns: `pass1` = pass@1 (%) [unit: %] (higher is better); `compile` = Compile rate (%) [unit: %] (higher is better)
| row_id | label | pass1 | compile |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Ridgeline-FT (ours) | 47.9 ± 1.9 | 93.1 ± 1.3 |
| r2 | Plainweave-FT | 45.6 ± 1.7 | 90.4 ± 1.8 |
| r3 | Fernleaf-1.3B (no fine-tuning) | 31.4 | 84.0 |

## Statistical tests
- `S1` for comparison `exp1/cmp1`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=8 random seeds (paired by seed); t=5.33 (df=7); Holm-adjusted p = 0.0022; 95% CI [1.26, 3.26] for mean paired difference in pass@1 (Ridgeline-FT − Plainweave-FT), percentage points; unadjusted 95% CI; alpha=0.05; adjustment: Holm–Bonferroni across the two pre-specified endpoints (S1 pass@1, S2 compile rate); note: Unadjusted p = 0.0011.
- `S2` for comparison `exp1/cmp2`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=8 random seeds (paired by seed); t=4.14 (df=7); Holm-adjusted p = 0.0043; 95% CI [1.19, 4.35] for mean paired difference in compile rate (Ridgeline-FT − Plainweave-FT), percentage points; unadjusted 95% CI; alpha=0.05; adjustment: Holm–Bonferroni across the two pre-specified endpoints (S1 pass@1, S2 compile rate); note: Unadjusted p = 0.0043 (unchanged by the Holm step-down).

39. S949d8beb — case K05

# Case K05

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Corbel's zero-shot retrieval gains compare with the improvements previously reported for denoised hard-negative mining?

## Methods summary
Corbel is a dense bi-encoder retriever trained with denoised hard-negative mining (Hallberg & Osei, 2023), which is commonly cited as improving zero-shot nDCG@10 by about 5 points across diverse domains; Corbel adds query-side length normalization. Dovetail-DR is the same bi-encoder trained with standard in-batch negatives. Lexi-Sparse is a lexical reference retriever.

## Experimental setup
Both dense retrievers are trained on the same 480k query–passage pairs and evaluated zero-shot (no in-domain training) on four held-out collections described by Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021): Brackwater-News, Felloe-Fin, Moorstone-Bio and Lintel-Law. Metric: nDCG@10 (×100; higher is better); single training run per system.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Zero-shot retrieval on four held-out collections
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Corbel vs Dovetail-DR: nDCG@10 per collection and average
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Corbel vs Lexi-Sparse: nDCG@10 per collection and average

## Results tables
### Table T1: Zero-shot nDCG@10 (×100) on four held-out collections.
Columns: `news` = Brackwater-News (higher is better); `fin` = Felloe-Fin (higher is better); `bio` = Moorstone-Bio (higher is better); `law` = Lintel-Law (higher is better); `avg` = Average (higher is better)
| row_id | label | news | fin | bio | law | avg |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Lexi-Sparse | 40.2 | 27.9 | 41.3 | 31.8 | 35.3 |
| r2 | Dovetail-DR | 38.4 | 31.2 | 44.7 | 29.7 | 36.0 |
| r3 | Corbel (ours) | 43.1 | 35.0 | 50.6 | 34.1 | 40.7 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Hallberg & Osei (2023), "Cleaner Negatives: Denoised Hard-Negative Mining for Zero-Shot Dense Retrieval":
  > Averaged over the six zero-shot collections in our suite, which span news, finance, biomedical, legal, forum and scientific text, denoised hard-negative mining raised nDCG@10 by 5.2 points over the same retriever trained with standard in-batch negatives, with the largest gains on queries longer than ten tokens.
- `P2` — Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021), "Four Held-Out Collections for Zero-Shot Retrieval Evaluation":
  > Brackwater-News, Felloe-Fin, Moorstone-Bio and Lintel-Law were assembled from public-domain sources and contain between 600 and 1,400 test queries each; relevance judgments were pooled from five retrieval systems and graded on a 0–3 scale.

40. S60541145 — case K19

On the Garnet-Retail test split, Thimble's long-tail Recall@20 of 0.091 exceeds both Cobble-GNN's 0.072 and Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling's 0.085, corresponding to relative gains of 26.4% and 7.1%, respectively. The 18.1% relative improvement in long-tail Recall@20 from Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling (0.085 vs 0.072) is consistent with the roughly 20% relative gains reported by Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022) for popularity-debiased negative sampling, though that prior work used different datasets. Thimble's overall Recall@20 (0.219) is only slightly higher than Cobble-GNN's (0.214) and Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling's (0.212), with relative differences of 2.3% and 3.3%, respectively. No statistical tests were reported, so these differences, while directionally consistent with the expected benefits of debiased sampling, should be interpreted cautiously on this single dataset.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K19

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

## Results tables
### Table T1: Recall@20 on the Garnet-Retail test split (mean of 3 runs).
Columns: `overall` = Recall@20 (overall) (higher is better); `tail` = Recall@20 (long-tail) (higher is better)
| row_id | label | overall | tail |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Cobble-GNN (uniform sampling) | 0.214 | 0.072 |
| r2 | Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling | 0.212 | 0.085 |
| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022), "Sampling Against the Head: Popularity-Debiased Negatives for Recommendation":
  > On both datasets we studied, popularity-debiased negative sampling improved Recall@20 on long-tail items (the 80% least popular items) by 21% relative to uniform negative sampling. Overall Recall@20 changed by less than 1% on both datasets.
- `P2` — Imre & Castellanos (2020), "Garnet-Retail: A Session-Rich Benchmark for Retail Recommendation":
  > Garnet-Retail contains 2.3 million interactions from 41,000 users over 18,500 items collected over 26 weeks; the last two weeks of each user's history form the test split.

44. S561dc819 — case K26

Stellate-Net meets the pre-specified robustness criterion at all three tested blur severities, with top-1 accuracy drops of 0.7, 1.6, and 2.4 points at σ = 1, 2, and 3 px, respectively, all within the 3.0-point threshold. In contrast, Lattice-CNN exceeds the 3.0-point threshold at severities 3 and 5, with drops of 5.7 and 11.3 points, respectively, while remaining within the threshold at severity 1 (1.8 points). These results indicate that Stellate-Net is robust to Gaussian blur under the specified criterion, whereas the comparable Lattice-CNN is not, but the absence of statistical tests precludes claims of significance.

# Case K26

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Stellate-Net robust to Gaussian blur in its input images?

## Methods summary
Stellate-Net is a convolutional land-cover classifier trained with frequency-mixing augmentation. Lattice-CNN is a standard convolutional classifier of comparable size (23M vs 24M parameters) trained on the same data with the same schedule.

## Experimental setup
Both models are trained on the Ostmark-Aerial training split (24,000 images, 10 land-cover classes) and evaluated by top-1 accuracy on its 6,000-image test split; accuracies are means over 3 seeds. Blur study: test images are blurred with a Gaussian kernel at severity 1 (σ = 1 px), severity 3 (σ = 2 px) and severity 5 (σ = 3 px); blurred images are never used for training or tuning, and the frequency-mixing augmentation does not include blur. Pre-specified robustness criterion: a model counts as robust at a severity if its top-1 accuracy drops by at most 3.0 points from its clean accuracy.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Clean and blurred evaluation on the Ostmark-Aerial test split
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Stellate-Net: accuracy drop from clean at each blur severity
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Lattice-CNN: accuracy drop from clean at each blur severity

46. Sfa6869d4 — case K22

On the Garnet-Retail test split, Thimble achieves a long-tail Recall@20 of 0.091, compared with 0.085 for Cobble-GNN with debiased sampling and 0.072 for Cobble-GNN with uniform sampling. Adding popularity-debiased negative sampling to Cobble-GNN improves long-tail Recall@20 from 0.072 to 0.085, a relative gain of about 18% on this test split. Thimble's overall Recall@20 (0.219) is slightly higher than Cobble-GNN's (0.214) and Cobble-GNN with debiased sampling (0.212), but no statistical tests were reported, so these differences may not be significant.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K22

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

## Results tables
### Table T1: Recall@20 on the Garnet-Retail test split (mean of 3 runs).
Columns: `overall` = Recall@20 (overall) (higher is better); `tail` = Recall@20 (long-tail) (higher is better)
| row_id | label | overall | tail |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Cobble-GNN (uniform sampling) | 0.214 | 0.072 |
| r2 | Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling | 0.212 | 0.085 |
| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Sorensen-Nakata et al. (2022), "Sampling Against the Head: Popularity-Debiased Negatives for Recommendation":
  > On both datasets we studied, popularity-debiased negative sampling reduced training time per epoch by 21% relative to uniform negative sampling, because negatives are drawn from a precomputed alias table. Overall Recall@20 changed by less than 1% on both datasets.
- `P2` — Imre & Castellanos (2020), "Garnet-Retail: A Session-Rich Benchmark for Retail Recommendation":
  > Garnet-Retail contains 2.3 million interactions from 41,000 users over 18,500 items collected over 26 weeks; the last two weeks of each user's history form the test split.

## Research question
How does Bramblewood compare with Larkspur-Seq in NDCG@10 on the Brightcart-Grocery dataset?

## Methods summary

Larkspur-Seq, a self-attentive sequential recommender, is the strongest baseline in our study; Most-Popular, which recommends globally popular items, is a non-personalized reference.

## Experimental setup
Models are trained on the first 26 days of Brightcart-Grocery logs (1.2M sessions) and evaluated on sessions from the final 4 days (61,400 sessions) with leave-last-item-out next-item prediction over the full item catalogue. Hyperparameters were tuned on a validation split from the training period. Bramblewood and Larkspur-Seq were each trained with 10 random seeds (seeds 0–9); seed i fixes initialization and mini-batch order for both models, so their seed-level scores are paired. Most-Popular is deterministic.

50. Sc079e400 — case K12

On the Kestrel-Grid test split, Eddyline achieved lower MAE than Brenna-ODE (0.212 vs 0.219) while using substantially fewer function evaluations (17.8 vs 64 NFE per window) and lower latency (112 ms vs 241 ms per batch of 256). Show research package and rubric
# Case K12

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Eddyline compare with Brenna-ODE in inference efficiency and forecast accuracy on Kestrel-Grid?

## Methods summary
Eddyline and Brenna-ODE share the same neural-ODE vector field (a 3-layer MLP) for 96-step-ahead electricity-load forecasting. Brenna-ODE integrates it with a fixed-step fourth-order Runge–Kutta solver (64 function evaluations, NFE, per forecast window). Eddyline replaces the fixed schedule with a learned step-size controller that needs far fewer evaluations; each Eddyline step additionally runs the controller network and an implicit correction solve, so one Eddyline step costs more than one RK4 stage. Sluice-TCN is a non-ODE convolutional forecaster included for reference.

## Experimental setup
Kestrel-Grid test split: 8,760 hourly forecast windows from 320 substations. Accuracy is mean absolute error (MAE, normalized load units; lower is better). We measured latency for all three models ourselves on one Halden H5 accelerator (fp32, batch size 256 windows, same software stack) as the median wall-clock time per batch over 50 timed batches after 10 warm-up batches. NFE is averaged over test windows.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Inference cost and accuracy on Kestrel-Grid test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Eddyline vs Brenna-ODE: latency, NFE and MAE
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Eddyline vs Sluice-TCN: latency and MAE

## Results tables
### Table T1: Forecast accuracy and inference cost on the Kestrel-Grid test split.
Columns: `mae` = MAE (lower is better); `nfe` = NFE per window (lower is better); `lat` = Latency (ms per batch of 256) [unit: ms] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | mae | nfe | lat |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Eddyline (ours) | 0.212 | 17.8 | 112 |
| r2 | Brenna-ODE (RK4, 64 NFE) | 0.219 | 64 | 241 |
| r3 | Sluice-TCN | 0.231 | — | 38 |

# Case K24

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Does Halyard outperform Tidewell across the forecasting benchmark suite?

## Methods summary
Halyard is a patch-based transformer forecaster with per-series normalization. Tidewell, a seasonal-trend decomposition forecaster, is the strongest baseline in our study; seasonal-naive (repeating the value from one season earlier) is a reference.

## Experimental setup
The suite contains five multivariate forecasting datasets: Kelso-Grid (electricity load), Marrick-Traffic (road occupancy), Doveton-Weather (meteorological sensors), Ashby-Retail (store sales) and Pell-Solar (photovoltaic output). All methods forecast 96 steps ahead from a 336-step input window, use chronological 70/10/20 splits and receive the same tuning budget on the validation split. Metric: test MAE on z-normalized series (lower is better), mean over 3 seeds, reported to three decimals.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Five-dataset forecasting suite (horizon 96)
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Halyard vs Tidewell, test MAE, per dataset
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Halyard vs seasonal-naive, test MAE, per dataset

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Sablewing a more efficient sampler than Ordell-DDS for 256×256 face generation, and what does it cost in sample quality?

## Methods summary
Sablewing distils the Ordell-DDS denoiser into a student network that generates an image in 8 denoising steps (8 network function evaluations, NFE). The student is slightly wider than the teacher, so each of its evaluations costs somewhat more compute. Ordell-DDS is the 50-step teacher sampler; Ordell-DDS-10 runs the same sampler truncated to 10 steps.

## Experimental setup
Each sampler generates 30,000 images at 256×256 resolution for Halvern-Faces; FID is computed against the full Halvern-Faces reference set (lower is better). NFE counts denoiser evaluations per image, and TFLOPs per image are computed analytically from the network architecture and NFE. All models use fp16 weights.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Sampler comparison on Halvern-Faces 256×256
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS (50 steps): sampling cost and FID
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS-10: sampling cost and FID

| row_id | label | fid | nfe | tflops |

## Methods summary

Larkspur-Seq, a self-attentive sequential recommender, is the strongest baseline in our study; Most-Popular, which recommends globally popular items, is a non-personalized reference.

## Experimental setup
Models are trained on the first 26 days of Brightcart-Grocery logs (1.2M sessions) and evaluated on sessions from the final 4 days (61,400 sessions) with leave-last-item-out next-item prediction over the full item catalogue. Bramblewood and Larkspur-Seq were each trained with 10 random seeds (seeds 0–9); seed i fixes initialization and mini-batch order for both models, so their seed-level scores are paired. Most-Popular is deterministic.

# Case K27

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Corbel's zero-shot retrieval gains compare with the improvements previously reported for denoised hard-negative mining?

## Methods summary
Corbel is a dense bi-encoder retriever trained with denoised hard-negative mining (Hallberg & Osei, 2023), which is commonly cited as improving zero-shot nDCG@10 by about 5 points across diverse domains; Corbel adds query-side length normalization. Dovetail-DR is the same bi-encoder trained with standard in-batch negatives. Lexi-Sparse is a lexical reference retriever.

## Experimental setup
Both dense retrievers are trained on the same 480k query–passage pairs and evaluated zero-shot (no in-domain training) on four held-out collections described by Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021): Brackwater-News, Felloe-Fin, Moorstone-Bio and Lintel-Law. Metric: nDCG@10 (×100; higher is better); single training run per system.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Zero-shot retrieval on four held-out collections
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Corbel vs Dovetail-DR: nDCG@10 per collection and average
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Corbel vs Lexi-Sparse: nDCG@10 per collection and average

## Results tables
### Table T1: Zero-shot nDCG@10 (×100) on four held-out collections.
Columns: `news` = Brackwater-News (higher is better); `fin` = Felloe-Fin (higher is better); `bio` = Moorstone-Bio (higher is better); `law` = Lintel-Law (higher is better); `avg` = Average (higher is better)
| row_id | label | news | fin | bio | law | avg |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Lexi-Sparse | 40.2 | 27.9 | 41.3 | 31.8 | 35.3 |
| r2 | Dovetail-DR | 38.4 | 31.2 | 44.7 | 29.7 | 36.0 |
| r3 | Corbel (ours) | 43.1 | 35.0 | 50.6 | 34.1 | 40.7 |

## Statistical tests
(no statistical tests reported)

## Cited source passages
- `P1` — Hallberg & Osei (2023), "Cleaner Negatives: Denoised Hard-Negative Mining for Zero-Shot Dense Retrieval":
  > On the biomedical collection in our suite, denoised hard-negative mining raised nDCG@10 by 5.2 points over the same retriever trained with standard in-batch negatives, with the largest gains on queries longer than ten tokens.
- `P2` — Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021), "Four Held-Out Collections for Zero-Shot Retrieval Evaluation":

# Case K21

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Bramblewood compare with Larkspur-Seq in NDCG@10 on the Brightcart-Grocery dataset?

## Methods summary
Bramblewood is a session-based recommender that builds an item-transition graph for each session and scores candidate items with a gated graph encoder. Larkspur-Seq, a self-attentive sequential recommender, is the strongest baseline in our study; Most-Popular, which recommends globally popular items, is a non-personalized reference.

## Experimental setup
Models are trained on the first 26 days of Brightcart-Grocery logs (1.2M sessions) and evaluated on sessions from the final 4 days (61,400 sessions) with leave-last-item-out next-item prediction over the full item catalogue. Hyperparameters were tuned on a validation split from the training period. Bramblewood and Larkspur-Seq were each trained with 10 random seeds (seeds 0–9); seed i fixes initialization and mini-batch order for both models, so their seed-level scores are paired. Most-Popular is deterministic.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Next-item prediction on the Brightcart-Grocery test period
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Bramblewood vs Larkspur-Seq, NDCG@10
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Bramblewood vs Most-Popular, NDCG@10

| row_id | label | ndcg | recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Bramblewood (ours) | 41.2 ± 0.7 | 63.8 ± 0.8 |
| r2 | Larkspur-Seq | 40.7 ± 0.6 | 63.1 ± 0.8 |
| r3 | Most-Popular | 18.3 | 37.9 |

## Statistical tests
- `S1` for comparison `exp1/cmp1`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=10 random seeds (paired by seed); t=3.79 (df=9); p = 0.0043; 95% CI [0.21, 0.85] for mean paired difference in NDCG@10 (Bramblewood − Larkspur-Seq), percentage points; alpha=0.05

Show research package and rubric
# Case K14

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Ridgeline-FT compare with Plainweave-FT in pass@1 on Quillbench?

## Methods summary
Ridgeline-FT fine-tunes a code model with an execution-feedback curriculum that orders training problems by the failure rate of the model's own samples. Plainweave-FT is standard supervised fine-tuning on the same data. Both start from the same 1.3B-parameter base model, Fernleaf-1.3B, which is also reported without fine-tuning.

## Experimental setup
Both recipes fine-tune Fernleaf-1.3B on the same 85,000 problem–solution pairs, each with 8 random seeds (seeds 0–7); seed i fixes data order and initialization for both recipes, so seed-level scores are paired. Evaluation uses greedy decoding on all 420 Quillbench problems (Python, hidden unit tests). The analysis plan pre-specified two endpoints for Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT, pass@1 (primary) and compile rate (secondary), and controls the family-wise error rate across these two tests with the Holm–Bonferroni procedure at α = 0.05.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT on Quillbench (420 problems)
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT, pass@1 (primary endpoint)
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Ridgeline-FT vs Plainweave-FT, compile rate (secondary endpoint)

## Results tables
### Table T1: Quillbench results, mean ± SD over 8 seeds (base model: single deterministic run).
Columns: `pass1` = pass@1 (%) [unit: %] (higher is better); `compile` = Compile rate (%) [unit: %] (higher is better)
| row_id | label | pass1 | compile |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Ridgeline-FT (ours) | 47.9 ± 1.9 | 93.1 ± 1.3 |
| r2 | Plainweave-FT | 45.6 ± 1.7 | 90.4 ± 1.8 |
| r3 | Fernleaf-1.3B (no fine-tuning) | 31.4 | 84.0 |

## Statistical tests
- `S1` for comparison `exp1/cmp1`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=8 random seeds (paired by seed); t=5.33 (df=7); Holm-adjusted p = 0.0022; 95% CI [1.26, 3.26] for mean paired difference in pass@1 (Ridgeline-FT − Plainweave-FT), percentage points; unadjusted 95% CI; alpha=0.05; adjustment: Holm–Bonferroni across the two pre-specified endpoints (S1 pass@1, S2 compile rate); note: Unadjusted p = 0.0011.
- `S2` for comparison `exp1/cmp2`: paired t-test; two-sided; paired; n=8 random seeds (paired by seed); t=4.14 (df=7); Holm-adjusted p = 0.0043; 95% CI [1.19, 4.35] for mean paired difference in compile rate (Ridgeline-FT − Plainweave-FT), percentage points; unadjusted 95% CI; alpha=0.05; adjustment: Holm–Bonferroni across the two pre-specified endpoints (S1 pass@1, S2 compile rate); note: Unadjusted p = 0.0043 (unchanged by the Holm step-down).

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Does Calloway-Sum's advantage over Pellam-Sum carry over to text domains other than news?

# Case K22

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

73. S05f83e81 — case K21

On the Brightcart-Grocery test set, Bramblewood achieved a mean NDCG@10 of 41.2% (SD 0.7) across 10 seeds, compared with 40.7% (SD 0.6) for Larkspur-Seq. A paired t-test (n=10, paired by seed) showed that this difference was statistically significant (t=3.79, df=9, p=0.0043, 95% CI [0.21, 0.85] percentage points), indicating that Bramblewood outperformed Larkspur-Seq in NDCG@10 on this dataset. # Case K21

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How does Bramblewood compare with Larkspur-Seq in NDCG@10 on the Brightcart-Grocery dataset?

## Methods summary
Bramblewood is a session-based recommender that builds an item-transition graph for each session and scores candidate items with a gated graph encoder. Larkspur-Seq, a self-attentive sequential recommender, is the strongest baseline in our study; Most-Popular, which recommends globally popular items, is a non-personalized reference.

## Experimental setup
Models are trained on the first 26 days of Brightcart-Grocery logs (1.2M sessions) and evaluated on sessions from the final 4 days (61,400 sessions) with leave-last-item-out next-item prediction over the full item catalogue. Hyperparameters were tuned on a validation split from the training period. Bramblewood and Larkspur-Seq were each trained with 10 random seeds (seeds 0–9); seed i fixes initialization and mini-batch order for both models, so their seed-level scores are paired. Most-Popular is deterministic.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Next-item prediction on the Brightcart-Grocery test period
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Bramblewood vs Larkspur-Seq, NDCG@10
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Bramblewood vs Most-Popular, NDCG@10

| row_id | label | ndcg | recall |
|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Bramblewood (ours) | 41.2 ± 0.7 | 63.8 ± 0.8 |

| r3 | Most-Popular | 18.3 | 37.9 |

76. Sfb5c2d13 — case K18

Sablewing is substantially more efficient than Ordell-DDS for 256×256 face generation, reducing NFE from 50 to 8, TFLOPs per image from 5.40 to 0.94, and wall-clock time from 21.7 s to 3.9 s per batch of 64, at the cost of a higher FID (7.8 vs. 7.1). No statistical tests were reported, so the observed differences in FID and sampling cost cannot be claimed as statistically significant.
Show research package and rubric
# Case K18

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
Is Sablewing a more efficient sampler than Ordell-DDS for 256×256 face generation, and what does it cost in sample quality?

## Methods summary
Sablewing distils the Ordell-DDS denoiser into a student network that generates an image in 8 denoising steps (8 network function evaluations, NFE). The student is slightly wider than the teacher, so each of its evaluations costs somewhat more compute. Ordell-DDS is the 50-step teacher sampler; Ordell-DDS-10 runs the same sampler truncated to 10 steps.

## Experimental setup
Each sampler generates 30,000 images at 256×256 resolution for Halvern-Faces; FID is computed against the full Halvern-Faces reference set (lower is better). NFE counts denoiser evaluations per image, and TFLOPs per image are computed analytically from the network architecture and NFE. All models use fp16 weights. Wall-clock sampling time was measured by us for all three samplers on one Vantor V3 accelerator (fp16, batch size 64, same software stack), as the median of 10 timed runs after 3 warm-up runs, and is reported in seconds per batch of 64 images.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Sampler comparison on Halvern-Faces 256×256
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS (50 steps): sampling cost and FID
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Sablewing vs Ordell-DDS-10: sampling cost and FID

## Results tables
### Table T1: Sample quality and sampling cost on Halvern-Faces 256×256.
Columns: `fid` = FID (lower is better); `nfe` = NFE (lower is better); `tflops` = TFLOPs per image [unit: TFLOPs] (lower is better); `wall` = Wall-clock time (s per batch of 64) [unit: s] (lower is better)
| row_id | label | fid | nfe | tflops | wall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Sablewing (ours) | 7.8 | 8 | 0.94 | 3.9 |
| r2 | Ordell-DDS (50 steps) | 7.1 | 50 | 5.40 | 21.7 |
| r3 | Ordell-DDS-10 | 15.6 | 10 | 1.08 | 4.5 |

# Case K27

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Corbel's zero-shot retrieval gains compare with the improvements previously reported for denoised hard-negative mining?

## Methods summary
Corbel is a dense bi-encoder retriever trained with denoised hard-negative mining (Hallberg & Osei, 2023), which is commonly cited as improving zero-shot nDCG@10 by about 5 points across diverse domains; Corbel adds query-side length normalization. Dovetail-DR is the same bi-encoder trained with standard in-batch negatives. Lexi-Sparse is a lexical reference retriever.

## Experimental setup
Both dense retrievers are trained on the same 480k query–passage pairs and evaluated zero-shot (no in-domain training) on four held-out collections described by Nakashima-Reyes & Thorne (2021): Brackwater-News, Felloe-Fin, Moorstone-Bio and Lintel-Law. Metric: nDCG@10 (×100; higher is better); single training run per system.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Zero-shot retrieval on four held-out collections
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Corbel vs Dovetail-DR: nDCG@10 per collection and average
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Corbel vs Lexi-Sparse: nDCG@10 per collection and average

Columns: `news` = Brackwater-News (higher is better); `fin` = Felloe-Fin (higher is better); `bio` = Moorstone-Bio (higher is better); `law` = Lintel-Law (higher is better); `avg` = Average (higher is better)

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Lexi-Sparse | 40.2 | 27.9 | 41.3 | 31.8 | 35.3 |
| r2 | Dovetail-DR | 38.4 | 31.2 | 44.7 | 29.7 | 36.0 |
| r3 | Corbel (ours) | 43.1 | 35.0 | 50.6 | 34.1 | 40.7 |

# Case K22

## Research package (exactly what the systems received)

## Research question
How do Thimble's long-tail recommendation results on Garnet-Retail relate to what prior work reports for popularity-debiased negative sampling?

## Methods summary
Thimble is a graph-based recommender built on Cobble-GNN. It adopts popularity-debiased negative sampling (Sorensen-Nakata et al., 2022), which is widely credited with roughly 20% relative gains in long-tail Recall@20, and adds a session-aware item encoder. 'Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling' applies only the sampling change.

## Experimental setup
Garnet-Retail (Imre & Castellanos, 2020) test split. Long-tail items are the 80% least-interacted items in training. Recall@20 is computed over all target items (overall) and over long-tail target items only (long-tail); higher is better. Values are means over 3 runs. Training time was not measured.

## Experiments and comparisons
- `exp1`: Recommendation accuracy on Garnet-Retail test
  - comparison `exp1/cmp1`: Thimble vs Cobble-GNN: overall and long-tail Recall@20
  - comparison `exp1/cmp2`: Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling vs Cobble-GNN: long-tail Recall@20

|---|---|---|---|
| r1 | Cobble-GNN (uniform sampling) | 0.214 | 0.072 |
| r2 | Cobble-GNN + debiased sampling | 0.212 | 0.085 |
| r3 | Thimble (ours) | 0.219 | 0.091 |

- `P2` — Imre & Castellanos (2020), "Garnet-Retail: A Session-Rich Benchmark for Retail Recommendation":
  > Garnet-Retail contains 2.3 million interactions from 41,000 users over 18,500 items collected over 26 weeks; the last two weeks of each user's history form the test split.

On the Kestrel-Grid test split, Eddyline achieved a lower MAE than Brenna-ODE (0.212 vs. 0.219) while requiring only 17.8 function evaluations per window compared to Brenna-ODE's fixed 64, and it also reduced median batch latency from 241 ms to 112 ms.
